These mixing matrices and vectors encode how the temporal and spatial components couple to one another and how the Kerr geometry distinguishes scalar and vector fields.

Boundary conditions

So far, the radial functions
F_{r}
,
F_{\theta}
,
F_{t}
have not been specified.
We are free to choose these functions in such a way that all of the components
A_{a}

scale in the same way as
r\to r_{+}
and
r\to\infty
. This will make it easy to impose the ingoing boundary conditions at the outer horizon and the decaying boundary conditions at spatial infinity.

Let us first concentrate on the behavior at the event horizon, which will be easiest to analyze using the standard form of the Proca equation
\nabla^{2}A_{\mu}=A_{\mu}
instead of the decomposition (
8
). We demand that
A_{\mu}
is an eigenstate of both frequency
\pounds_{t}A_{\mu}=-\omega A_{\mu}
and angular momentum,
\pounds_{z}A_{\mu}=mA_{\mu}
, and so the coefficients
A_{\mu}
can all be written as
A_{\mu}=A_{\mu}(r,\theta)\,e^{-i\omega t+im\phi}
. We can then solve the Lorenz condition for
A_{0}(r,\theta)
and eliminate it from the Proca equation to attain equations of motion that only involve the
A_{i}
. While the system of equations is quite complicated, for what follows, we only need to consider the asymptotic behavior of these equations as
r\to r_{+}
. We will thus use
(\,\dots)
to denote functions that are constant in
r
(though they generally depend on
\theta
,
r_{\pm}
,
m
and
\omega
) and whose precise form are irrelevant to the discussion.

With the benefit of hindsight, we take

A_{r}=\left(\frac{r-r_{-}}{r-r_{+}}\right)\tilde{A}_{r}\,,\quad A_{\theta}=(r-r_{-})\tilde{A}_{\theta}\,,\quad A_{\phi}=(r-r_{-})\tilde{A}_{\phi}\,.

(12)

With these factors peeled off, the near-horizon behavior for the
r
,
\theta
,
\phi
components of the Proca equation are

\displaystyle 0\,=\,\

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{2}\tilde{A}_{r}+\frac{1}{r-r_{+}}\partial_{r}\tilde{A}_{r}+\frac{P_{+}^{2}}{(r-r_{+})^{2}}\tilde{A}_{r}+\frac{(\,\dots)}{r-r_{+}}\partial_{\theta}\tilde{A}_{\theta}+\frac{(\,\dots)}{r-r_{+}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}\tilde{A}_{r}

\displaystyle+(\,\dots)\partial_{r}\tilde{A}_{\theta}+\frac{(\,\dots)}{r-r_{+}}\partial_{\theta}\tilde{A}_{r}+\frac{(\,\dots)}{r-r_{+}}\tilde{A}_{\phi}+\frac{(\,\dots)}{r-r_{+}}\tilde{A}_{\theta}+\cdots\,,

(13)

\displaystyle 0\,=\,\

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{2}\tilde{A}_{\theta}+\frac{1}{r-r_{+}}\partial_{r}\tilde{A}_{\theta}+\frac{P_{+}^{2}}{(r-r_{+})^{2}}\tilde{A}_{\theta}+\frac{(\,\dots)}{r-r_{+}}\partial_{\theta}\tilde{A}_{r}+\frac{(\,\dots)}{r-r_{+}}\partial_{\theta}\tilde{A}_{\theta}

\displaystyle+\frac{(\,\dots)}{r-r_{+}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}\tilde{A}_{\theta}+\frac{(\,\dots)}{r-r_{+}}\tilde{A}_{\phi}+(\,\dots)\partial_{r}\tilde{A}_{r}+(\,\dots)\tilde{A}_{r}+\cdots\,,

(14)

\displaystyle 0\,=\ \,

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{2}\tilde{A}_{\phi}+\frac{1}{r-r_{+}}\partial_{r}\tilde{A}_{\phi}+\frac{P_{+}^{2}}{(r-r_{+})^{2}}\tilde{A}_{\phi}+\frac{(\,\dots)}{r-r_{+}}\tilde{A}_{\theta}+(\,\dots)\partial_{r}\tilde{A}_{\theta}+(\,\dots)\partial_{\theta}\tilde{A}_{r}

\displaystyle+\left(\,\dots\right)\left(\partial_{r}^{2}\tilde{A}_{r}+\frac{1}{r-r_{+}}\partial_{r}\tilde{A}_{r}+\frac{P_{+}^{2}}{(r-r_{+})^{2}}\tilde{A}_{r}\right)+(\,\dots)\partial_{r}\partial_{\theta}\tilde{A}_{\theta}

\displaystyle+\frac{(\,\dots)}{r-r_{+}}\partial_{\theta}\tilde{A}_{\theta}+\frac{(\,\dots)}{r-r_{+}}\partial_{\theta}\tilde{A}_{\phi}+(\,\dots)\partial_{\theta}^{2}\tilde{A}_{\theta}+\frac{(\,\dots)}{(r-r_{+})}\partial_{\theta}^{2}\tilde{A}_{\phi}+\cdots\,.

(15)

We see that most singular terms imply that
\tilde{A}_{r}
,
\tilde{A}_{\theta}
,
\tilde{A}_{\phi}
all go as
(r-r_{+})^{\pm iP_{+}}
as
r\to r_{+}
. In a similar way, we can also examine the Lorenz condition as
r\to r_{+}
,

\displaystyle A_{0}

\displaystyle=(\,\dots)(r-r_{+})\tilde{A}_{r}

(16)

\displaystyle+(r-r_{+})\partial_{r}\tilde{A}_{r}+(\,\dots)(r-r_{+})\partial_{\theta}\tilde{A}_{\theta}+(\,\dots)(r-r_{+})\tilde{A}_{\theta}+(\,\dots)\tilde{A}_{\phi}\,,

and we see that
A_{0}
also approaches
(r-r_{+})^{\pm iP_{+}}
as
r\to r_{+}
. With these scalings in mind, we take the radial functions to be

F_{t}=1\,,\quad F_{r}=\frac{r-r_{+}}{r-r_{-}}\,,\quad F_{\theta}=\frac{1}{r-r_{-}}\,,

(17)

so that
A_{a}\sim(r-r_{+})^{\pm iP_{+}}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(r-r_{+})\right)
as
r\to r_{+}
for each
a
.

To analyze how the coefficients
A_{a}
behave as
r\to\infty
, we turn to the decomposed Proca equation (
8
), where the mixing matrices are computed with the radial functions (
17
).
Ignoring the mixing matrices, for a moment, (
8
) predicts that the
A_{a}
scale in the same way as the scalar solution (
6
):

A_{a}\sim r^{-1-\nu+2\alpha^{2}/\nu}e^{-\sqrt{1-\omega^{2}}(r-r_{+})}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(r^{-1})\right).

(18)

This conclusion will not be affected by including the mixing matrices, as long as they
decay faster at spatial infinity than
r^{-1}
and, by explicit computation, we confirm that
\mathcal{S}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}_{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}=0
,
\{\mathcal{S}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}_{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}},\mathcal{S}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}},\mathcal{R}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}_{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}},\mathcal{R}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\}
scale as
r^{-2}
,
\{\mathcal{S}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}},\mathcal{R}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}_{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}},\mathcal{R}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}},\mathcal{P}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}_{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}},\mathcal{P}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}},\mathcal{Z}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}_{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}},\mathcal{Z}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}},\mathcal{M}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}_{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}},\mathcal{M}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\}
scale as
r^{-3}
, while the rest decay as
r^{-4}
.

We have chosen the radial functions (
17
), so that the components
A_{a}
all have the same asymptotic behavior as
r\to r_{+}
and
r\to\infty
. We then peel off this asymptotic behavior by writing

A_{a}(r)=\left(\frac{r-r_{+}}{r-r_{-}}\right)^{iP_{+}}\!\!\!\left(r-r_{-}\right)^{-1+\nu-2\alpha^{2}/\nu}e^{-\alpha(r-r_{+})/\nu}B_{a}(r)\,,

(19)

and work with the functions
B_{a}
, which, for the modes of interest, approach constants as
r\to r_{+}
and
r\to\infty
. In the numerics, this allows us to impose the boundary conditions for the quasi-bound states by simply expanding
B_{a}
in a set of functions that approach constant values at the boundaries.

Constructing the matrix equation

To convert (
8
) into a finite-dimensional matrix equation, we first write (
19
) as

\displaystyle A_{a}(r)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{r-r_{+}}{r-r_{-}}\right)^{iP_{+}}\!

(20)

\displaystyle\times(r-r_{-})^{-1+\nu-2\alpha^{2}/\nu}e^{-\alpha(r-r_{+})/\nu}B_{a}(\zeta(r))\equiv F(r)B_{a}(\zeta(r))\,,

which is similar to the scalar case (
9
), with
\zeta(r)
a map from
[r_{+},\infty)
to
[-1,1]
. The spatial components
i=1,2,3
of the Proca equation (
8
) can then be written as

\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}[B_{k}]+\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}[B_{0}]=0\,,

(21)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}

\displaystyle[B_{k}]\,=\,\ \Bigg(\partial_{\zeta}^{2}+\frac{1}{\zeta^{\prime}(r)}\left(\frac{1}{r-r_{+}}+\frac{1}{r-r_{-}}+\frac{2F^{\prime}(r)}{F(r)}+\frac{\zeta^{\prime\prime}(r)}{\zeta^{\prime}(r)}\right)\partial_{\zeta}\Bigg)B_{i}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\zeta^{\prime}(r)^{2}\Delta}\left(\pounds^{2}+\alpha^{2}\tilde{a}^{2}\left(1-\omega^{2}\right)\cos^{2}\theta\right)B_{i}+\frac{1}{\zeta^{\prime}(r)^{2}}\Bigg(\frac{F^{\prime}/F}{r-r_{+}}

\displaystyle+\frac{F^{\prime}/F}{r-r_{-}}+\frac{F^{\prime\prime}}{F}-(1-\omega^{2})+\frac{P_{+}^{2}}{(r-r_{+})^{2}}+\frac{P_{-}^{2}}{(r-r_{-})^{2}}

(22)

\displaystyle-\frac{A_{+}}{(r-r_{+})(r_{+}-r_{-})}+\frac{A_{-}}{(r-r_{-})(r_{+}-r_{-})}\Bigg)B_{i}+\tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}B_{k}

\displaystyle+\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\,\pounds_{z}B_{k}+\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\,\partial_{\zeta}B_{k}+\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\,\mathcal{D}_{+}B_{k}+\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\,\mathcal{D}_{0}B_{k}+\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\,\mathcal{D}_{-}B_{k}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}[B_{0}]\,=\,\ \tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}B_{0}+\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\pounds_{z}B_{0}+\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\partial_{\zeta}B_{i}

\displaystyle\hskip 40.0pt+\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\,\mathcal{D}_{+}B_{0}+\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\,\mathcal{D}_{0}B_{0}+\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\,\mathcal{D}_{-}B_{0}\,.

(23)

In the above, we have defined the transformed mixing matrices

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\zeta^{\prime\hskip 1.0pt2}}\mathcal{S}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}+\frac{F^{\prime}}{\zeta^{\prime\hskip 1.0pt2}F}\mathcal{R}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\,,\quad\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}=\mathcal{R}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}/\zeta^{\prime}\,,\quad\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}=\mathcal{Q}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}/\zeta^{\prime\hskip 1.0pt2}\,,

(24)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}/\zeta^{\prime\hskip 1.0pt2}\,,\quad\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}=\mathcal{Z}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}/\zeta^{\prime\hskip 1.0pt2}\,,\quad\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}=\mathcal{M}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}/\zeta^{\prime\hskip 1.0pt2}\,,

where
\zeta^{\prime}=\partial_{r}\zeta
. Similarly, the Lorenz condition can be written as

\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}_{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}[B_{0}]+\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}_{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}[B_{i}]=0\,,

(25)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}_{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}[B_{0}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}^{0}B_{0}\,,

(26)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}_{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}[B_{i}]

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{i}B_{i}+\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{i}\partial_{\zeta}B_{i}+\mathcal{P}^{i}\mathcal{D}_{+}B_{i}+\mathcal{Z}^{i}\mathcal{D}_{0}B_{i}+\mathcal{M}^{i}\mathcal{D}_{-}B_{i}\,,

(27)

with
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{i}=\mathcal{S}^{i}+F^{\prime}\mathcal{R}^{i}/F
and
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{i}=\zeta^{\prime}\,\mathcal{R}^{i}
.

We then expand the temporal component of the field into scalar harmonics and the spatial components into one-form harmonics,

\displaystyle B_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j^{\prime}}B_{j^{\prime}m}Y_{j^{\prime}m}\,,

(28)

\displaystyle B_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell^{\prime},j^{\prime}}B_{\ell^{\prime},j^{\prime}m}\,Y_{i}^{\ell^{\prime},j^{\prime}m}\,.

(29)

As discussed in Appendix
8.A
, the summation ranges of
j^{\prime}
and
\ell^{\prime}
depend on the parity of the mode we are solving for.
We then project the Proca equation (
21
) onto the vector harmonics and the Lorenz constraint (
25
) onto the scalar harmonics, to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}^{{\mathrm{ss}}}_{\ell j\,|\,\ell^{\prime}j^{\prime}}[B_{\ell^{\prime},j^{\prime}m}(\zeta)]+\sum_{j^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}^{{\mathrm{st}}}_{\ell j\,|\,j^{\prime}}[B_{j^{\prime}m}(\zeta)]

\displaystyle=0\,,

(30)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}^{{\mathrm{ts}}}_{j\,|\,\ell^{\prime}j^{\prime}}[B_{\ell^{\prime},j^{\prime}m}(\zeta)]+\sum_{j^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}^{{\mathrm{tt}}}_{j\,|\,j^{\prime}}[B_{j^{\prime}m}(\zeta)]

\displaystyle=0\,.

(31)

Explicitly, we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{ss}}_{\ell j\,|\,\ell^{\prime}j^{\prime}}[B_{\ell^{\prime},j^{\prime}m}(\zeta)]

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathrm{S}}^{2}}\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,\,\bar{Y}^{i}_{\ell,jm}\,\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\big[B_{\ell^{\prime},j^{\prime}m}(\zeta)Y^{\ell^{\prime},j^{\prime}m}_{k}\big]\,,

(32)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{{\mathrm{st}}}_{\ell j\,|\,j^{\prime}}[B_{j^{\prime}m}(\zeta)]

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathrm{S}}^{2}}\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,\,\bar{Y}^{i}_{\ell,jm}\,\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\big[B_{j^{\prime}m}(\zeta)Y_{j^{\prime}\,m}\big]\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{{\mathrm{ts}}}_{j\,|\,\ell^{\prime}j^{\prime}}[B_{\ell^{\prime},j^{\prime}m}(\zeta)]

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathrm{S}}^{2}}\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,\,\bar{Y}_{jm}\,\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}}}_{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\big[B_{\ell^{\prime},j^{\prime}m}(\zeta)Y^{\ell^{\prime},j^{\prime}m}_{k}\big]\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{{\mathrm{tt}}}_{j\,|\,j^{\prime}}[B_{j^{\prime}m}(\zeta)]

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathrm{S}}^{2}}\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\,\,\bar{Y}_{jm}\,\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}_{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\big[B_{j^{\prime}m}(\zeta)Y_{j^{\prime}m}\big]\,.

Because we defined the
f^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}
to have definite angular momentum, the harmonics (
15
) and
26
) take simple forms in this tetrad basis. For instance, the components of the one-form harmonics are

Y^{\ell,jm}_{i}=\langle(1\,m_{i})\,(\ell\,\,m-m_{i})|j\,m\rangle\,Y_{\ell,m-m_{i}}(\theta,\phi)\,,

(33)

where
m_{i}=2-i
. In practice, the overlaps (
32
) can be computed efficiently, as each term in the rewritten Proca equation (
8
) operates very simply on these harmonics.

A vector field
is a solution of
the Proca equation and the Lorenz constraint if and only if (
30
) and (
31
) are satisfied for all
j
and
\ell
. Following the scalar case in §
4
, we may approximate the radial functions as

B_{\ell^{\prime},j^{\prime}m}(\zeta)=\sum_{k=0}^{N}B_{\ell^{\prime},j^{\prime}m}(\zeta_{k})\,p_{k}(\zeta)\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_{j^{\prime}m}=\sum_{k=0}^{N}B_{j^{\prime}m}(\zeta_{k})\,p_{k}(\zeta)\,,

(34)

where
p_{k}(\zeta)
are the cardinal polynomials associated to the points
\{\zeta_{k}\}
. By substituting these approximations into (
30
) and (
31
), sampling each equation at the
\{\zeta_{n}\}
, and truncating the angular expansion, we convert this system of equations into a finite-dimensional matrix equation. This matrix, whose structure is depicted in Fig.
14
, can then be passed to a nonlinear eigenvalue solver to determine the bound state spectrum.

Chebyshev interpolation

Our numerical techniques rely on approximating the scalar and vector field configurations using finite and discrete sets of data. This discretization is a necessary step towards approximating the Klein-Gordon and Proca equations—i.e. partial
differential
equations—as finite-dimensional matrix equations.
How we discretize matters immensely, and a careless choice can cause the numerics to fail outright. This section
explains both what Chebyshev interpolation is and why we choose it, and provides various technical details needed for the numerics outlined in §
3
. For an excellent introduction to the subject, see
[
527
]
.

Any smooth function
f(\zeta)
on the interval
\zeta\in[-1,1]
has a unique representation in terms of Chebyshev polynomials,

f(\zeta)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}T_{k}(\zeta)\,,

(35)

where the Chebyshev polynomials of degree
k
are
T_{k}(\cos\theta)=\cos k\theta
.
The Chebyshev coefficients are then given by

a_{k}=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\!\mathrm{d}\zeta\,\frac{T_{k}(\zeta)\,f(\zeta)}{\sqrt{1-\zeta^{2}}}\,,

(36)

for
k\geq 1
, while the right-hand side is multiplied by a factor of
1/2
when
k=0
. If we define an expanded function on the unit circle,

F(z)=F(1/z)=f(\zeta)
, with
\zeta=(z+z^{-1})/2
, then the Chebyshev expansion (
35
) is nothing more than the unique Laurent series for
F(z)
and (
36
) is simply a translation of the Cauchy integral formula.

With this expansion in hand, we may define a degree
N
polynomial approximation to
f(\zeta)
by truncating the sum,

\tilde{f}_{N}(\zeta)=\sum_{k=0}^{N}a_{k}T_{k}(\zeta)\,.

(37)

While this approximation is guaranteed to become exact as
N\to\infty
, its accuracy at finite
N
depends on how quickly the Chebyshev coefficients decay. This, in turn, depends on the analytic structure of
f(\zeta)
and thus
F(z)
. Since
f(\zeta)
is analytic on the interval
\zeta\in[-1,1]
, the expanded function
F(z)
is necessarily analytic within an annulus about the unit circle, the largest of which we denote
\rho^{-1}\leq|z|\leq\rho
. The Laurent series for
F(z)
diverges outside of this annulus, and so its size determines the asymptotic behavior of the Chebyshev coefficients, i.e.
a_{k}<\mathcal{C}\rho^{-k}
as
k\to\infty
for some constant
\mathcal{C}
. Since
|T_{k}(\zeta)|\leq 1
on the interval, the truncation error scales as

|f(\zeta)-\tilde{f}_{N}(\zeta)|\leq\sum_{k=N+1}^{\infty}\left|a_{k}T_{k}(\zeta)\right|\leq\frac{\mathcal{C}\rho^{-N}}{\rho-1}\,,\quad\text{as}\quad N\to\infty\,.

(38)

The Chebyshev approximation (
37
) thus converges to
f(\zeta)
exponentially quickly, at a rate set by the singularity closest to the unit circle
|z|=1
. If
F(z)
is entire, the approximation converges even faster.

Of course, we are mainly interested in the analytic structure of
f(\zeta)
, and not its expanded counterpart
F(z)
. However, we can relate the two by noting that
\zeta=(z+z^{-1})/2
maps a circle of radius
\rho
into an ellipse with foci at
\zeta=\pm 1
defined by

\zeta(\theta)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\rho+\frac{1}{\rho}\right)\cos\theta+\frac{i}{2}\left(\rho-\frac{1}{\rho}\right)\sin\theta\,.

(39)

This is called the Bernstein ellipse of radius
\rho
, depicted in Fig.
1
. From the above logic, the size of the largest such ellipse inside which
f(\zeta)
is analytic then determines how quickly the Chebyshev expansion (
35
) converges, and thus the number of terms needed to approximate
f(\zeta)
to a desired accuracy.

Figure 1:
The interval
\zeta\in[-1,1]
and Bernstein ellipses of various sizes. The distance between the closest singularity of the function
f(\zeta)
(pictured as
) and the finite interval determines how quickly the Chebyshev expansion and interpolation converge. As illustrated here, truncation errors roughly decay as
(3.2)^{-N}
.

In practice, we will use a different degree
N
polynomial approximation, called the
interpolant

f_{N}(\zeta)=\sum_{k=0}^{N}c_{k}T_{k}(\zeta)\,,

(40)

that will inherit the convergence properties of the projection (
37
), yet is more convenient and numerically robust. The
N+1
coefficients
c_{k}
are defined by requiring that
f_{N}(\zeta_{n})=f(\zeta_{n})
at a set of
N+1
interpolation points
\{\zeta_{n}\}
. We may rewrite this interpolant in terms of the degree
N

Lagrange polynomials

f_{N}(\zeta)=\sum_{k=0}^{N}f(\zeta_{k})\,p_{k}(\zeta)\,,

(41)

which are defined with respect to the interpolation points as

p_{k}(\zeta_{n})=\delta_{nk}\,.

(42)

Explicitly, the Lagrange polynomials are given by

p_{n}(\zeta)=\frac{\prod_{k\neq n}(\zeta-\zeta_{k})}{\prod_{k\neq n}(\zeta_{n}-\zeta_{k})}=\frac{p(\zeta)\,w_{n}}{\,(\zeta-\zeta_{n})}\,,

(43)

where we have defined both the degree
N+1

node polynomial

p(\zeta)=\prod_{k=0}^{N}(\zeta-\zeta_{n})\,

(44)

and the
weights

w_{n}^{-1}=p^{\prime}(\zeta_{n})
.

Whether or not this interpolant accurately approximates
f(\zeta)
away from the points
\{\zeta_{n}\}
depends crucially on how the interpolation points are distributed on the interval. One can show that the difference between the exact function and its approximation is given by
[
527
,
528
]

f(\zeta)-f_{N}(\zeta)=\frac{f^{(N+1)}(\zeta)\,p(\zeta)}{(N+1)!}\,.

(45)

If the interpolation points are such that the maximum value of
|p(\zeta)|
on the interval does not grow with its degree, then, for large enough
N
, this difference is guaranteed to be small everywhere on the interval. Given that the Chebyshev polynomial
T_{N+1}(\zeta)
is both extremely simple in its trigonometric form and is bounded on the interval by
\pm 1
for all
N
, we will take
p(\zeta)=T_{N+1}(\zeta)/2^{N}
and sample it at its zeros

\zeta_{k}=\cos\left(\frac{\pi(2k+1)}{2(N+1)}\right),\qquad k=0,\dots,N\,,

(46)

with corresponding weights

w_{k}=\sin\left(\frac{2\pi k(N+2)+\pi}{2(N+1)}\right),\qquad k=0,\dots,N\,.

(47)

The interpolant inherits the convergence properties (
38
) of the truncation if we interpolate with the
Chebyshev nodes
(
46
),
5
5

5

In fact, any set of interpolation points that are distributed according to the equilibrium distribution,
\rho(\zeta)=(\pi\sqrt{1-\zeta^{2}})^{-1}
, will share the convergence properties of the truncation (
37
). See
[
527
]
for more details.
and so (
41
) converges to
f(\zeta)
at rate set by the largest Bernstein ellipse.

In practice, evaluating the interpolant (
41
) away from the interpolation points using (
43
) is a numerical disaster
[
529
,
530
,
527
]
. Fortunately, the
second barycentric form
,

p_{n}(\zeta)=\frac{\lambda_{n}}{\zeta-\zeta_{n}}\,\Bigg/\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\zeta-\zeta_{k}}\,,

(48)

is numerically robust
[
531
,
529
]
and computationally efficient. This formula can be used to compute the derivative matrices
p_{k}^{\prime}(\zeta_{n})
and
p_{k}^{\prime\prime}(\zeta_{n})
that appear throughout the text in a stable way. Explicitly, we take

\displaystyle p^{\hskip 1.0pt\prime}_{k}(\zeta_{n})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{w_{k}/w_{n}}{\zeta_{n}-\zeta_{k}}&n\neq k\\
-\sum_{k\neq n}p^{\hskip 1.0pt\prime}_{k}(\zeta_{n})&n=k\end{cases}\,,

(49)

\displaystyle p^{\hskip 1.0pt\prime\prime}_{k}(\zeta_{n})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2p^{\hskip 1.0pt\prime}_{k}(\zeta_{n})p^{\hskip 1.0pt\prime}_{n}(\zeta_{n})-\frac{2p^{\hskip 1.0pt\prime}_{k}(\zeta_{n})}{\zeta_{n}-\zeta_{k}}&n\neq k\\
-\sum_{k\neq n}p^{\hskip 1.0pt\prime\prime}_{k}(\zeta_{n})&n=k\end{cases}\,.

(50)

Numerically, it is extremely important
[
532
]
to set the diagonal elements of these differentiation matrices such that the sum of all rows vanish identically. This forces the differentiation matrices to annihilate constant functions on the interval and can have an outsized effect on accuracy.

Gravitational Perturbations

In this appendix, we provide further details of the gravitational perturbations introduced in §
2
. We first present an alternative perspective of the derivation of (
2
) by considering a
three-body analogy
(Appendix
9.A
). We then show how a fictitous dipole in the gravitational potential, generated by a change of coordinates, cancels out (Appendix
9.B
). In addition, we generalize the tidal moments to inclined orbits (Appendix
9.C
) and present the next-to-leading order corrections to the gravitational potential (Appendix
9.D
).

Appendix 9.A
Three-Body Analogy

The eigenfunctions of the cloud are determined by the Hamiltonian of a test particle of mass
\mu
in the Schrödinger equation, cf. (
7
) and (
18
). This is analogous to the Hamiltonian of the electron in a hydrogen atom: while the electron wavefunction, as a solution of the Schrödinger equation, has a wave-like distribution around its nucleus, it is treated as a point particle at the level of the Hamiltonian. In the rest of this appendix, we will adopt this particle picture of the Hamiltonian.

Figure 1:
Coordinates of the effective three-body system.

In the presence of a companion of mass
M_{*}
, the cloud will accelerate due to the external gravitational field. The Hamiltonians in the Schrödinger equations (
7
) and (
18
) must hence be modified to include both the kinetic term of
M
as well as the contributions from
M_{*}
. It is convenient to introduce the center-of-mass coordinate,
\bm{\rho}\equiv(M{\bf{r}}_{1}+\mu{\bf{r}}_{2})/(M+\mu)
,
so that the total Hamiltonian
H_{\rm tot}
of the three-body system can be written as

H_{\rm tot}=\bigg[\frac{{\bf p}_{\rho}^{2}}{2(M+\mu)}+\frac{{\bf p}_{r}^{2}}{2\hat{\mu}}+V_{c}(|{\bf{r}}|)\bigg]+\bigg[\frac{{\bf p}_{*}^{2}}{2M_{*}}+V_{*}(\bar{\bf{r}},{\bf{R}}_{*})\bigg]\,,

(1)

where
{\bf{r}}\equiv{\bf{r}}_{2}-{\bf{r}}_{1}
is the relative spatial separation between
\mu
and
M
, and we introduced the reduced mass
\hat{\mu}\equiv M\mu/(M+\mu)
, the momenta
{\bf p}_{\rho}\equiv(M+\mu)\dot{\bm{\rho}}
and
{\bf p}_{r}\equiv\hat{\mu}\dot{{\bf{r}}}
, and the coordinates
{\bf R_{*}}\equiv\{R_{*},\Theta_{*},\Phi_{*}\}
and
\bar{\bf{r}}\equiv\{\bar{r},\bar{\theta},\bar{\phi}\}
relative to the center-of-mass (cf. Fig.
1
). Notice that we have used a different notation for the center-of-mass coordinates compared to that in the main text here. Working in the Newtonian limit, the external potential
V_{*}
is given by

\displaystyle V_{*}(\bar{\bf{r}},{\bf{R}}_{*})

\displaystyle=-\frac{M_{*}M}{|{\bf{R}}_{*}+\mu\bar{\bf{r}}/M|}-\frac{M_{*}\mu}{|{\bf{R}}_{*}-\bar{\bf{r}}|}

\displaystyle=-M_{*}\sum_{\ell_{*}m_{*}}\frac{4\pi}{2\ell_{*}+1}\left(\frac{M(-\mu/M)^{\ell_{*}}+\mu}{R_{*}}\right)\left(\frac{\bar{r}}{R_{*}}\right)^{\ell_{*}}\,

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt\times Y^{*}_{\ell_{*}m_{*}}(\Theta_{*},\Phi_{*})\,Y_{\ell_{*}m_{*}}(\bar{\theta},\bar{\phi})\,.

(2)

Substituting
\bar{\bf{r}}=(1+\mu/M)^{-1}\,{\bf{r}}
, and expanding in
\mu/M\ll 1
, we find

\displaystyle V_{*}(r,R_{*})

\displaystyle=-\frac{M_{*}(M+\mu)}{R_{*}}

\displaystyle-\frac{M_{*}\mu}{R_{*}}\,\sum_{\ell_{*}\geq 2}\sum_{|m_{*}|\leq\ell_{*}}\frac{4\pi}{2\ell_{*}+1}\left(\frac{r}{R_{*}}\right)^{\ell_{*}}\,Y^{*}_{\ell_{*}m_{*}}(\Theta_{*},\Phi_{*})\,Y_{\ell_{*}m_{*}}(\theta,\phi)\,,

\displaystyle\equiv V_{*,0}(R_{*})+\sum_{\ell_{*}\geq 2}V_{*,\ell_{*}}(r,R_{*})\,.

(3)

The leading monopole term
V_{*,0}
determines the acceleration of the center-of-mass of the cloud system. It doesn’t depend on the relative separation
{\bf{r}}
, so it doesn’t lead to a shift in the energy levels or the mode functions of the system. The remaining terms are a sum over harmonics, starting with the quadrupole
\ell_{*}=2
. Importantly, the dipole contribution
\ell_{*}=1
vanishes in the center-of-mass frame.

Appendix 9.B
Fictitious Dipole

In the previous subappendix, we have shown that the contribution from the dipole vanishes in the centre-of-mass frame. By virtue of the equivalence principle, this property must hold for all coordinate systems. More generally, this is a manifestation of the fact that a constant gravitational gradient — in this case the dipole term induced by the external gravitational field produced by the companion
M_{*}
— is physically unobservable. We now show explicitly that this is indeed the case for the coordinate system centered at
M
.

Consider expressing
H_{\rm tot}
in terms of
({\bf{r}}_{1},{\bf{r}})
instead of
(\bm{\rho},{\bf{r}})
,

\displaystyle H_{\rm tot}

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{{\bf p}_{*}^{2}}{2M_{*}}+V_{*}({\bf{r}},{\bf{R}})\right]

(1)

\displaystyle+\left[\left(1+\frac{\mu}{M}\right)\left(\frac{\textbf{p}_{1}^{2}}{2M}+\frac{\textbf{p}_{r}^{2}}{2\hat{\mu}}\right)+\frac{\mu}{M\hat{\mu}}\textbf{p}_{1}\cdot\textbf{p}_{r}+V_{c}(|{\bf{r}}|)\right]\,,

where the gravitational potential in this choice of coordinates reads (cf. Fig.
1
)

V_{*}({\bf{r}},{\bf{R}})=-\frac{M_{*}M}{|{\bf{R}}|}-\frac{M_{*}\mu}{|{\bf{R}}-{\bf{r}}|}\,.

(2)

Expanding
V_{*}
for
r\ll R
produces a dipole term

-\frac{M_{*}\mu}{|{\bf{R}}-\textbf{r}|}\supset-\left(\frac{M_{*}\mu}{R}\right)\left(\frac{\hat{\textbf{n}}\cdot\textbf{r}}{R}\right),\quad

(3)

where
\hat{\textbf{n}}
is the unit vector along
R
, and we have used
|\textbf{R}-\textbf{r}|=R-\hat{\textbf{n}}\cdot\textbf{r}+\mathcal{O}(r^{2})
.
We will now show that this dipole is cancelled by the kinetic mixing term
\mu\,\textbf{p}_{1}\cdot\textbf{p}_{r}/M\hat{\mu}
. This is manifested most transparently in the
M_{*}/R\gg V_{*}
limit, in which the gravitational attraction between
M
and
\mu
is negligible, compared to the force exerted by
M_{*}
. In this limit,
M
and
\mu
free fall separately under the gravitational influence of
M_{*}
:

\dot{\textbf{r}}^{2}_{1}=\frac{2M_{*}}{R}\,,\qquad\dot{\textbf{r}}^{2}_{2}=\frac{2M_{*}}{|\textbf{R}-\textbf{r}|}\,,

(4)

and the angle between
\dot{\textbf{r}}_{1}
and
\dot{\textbf{r}}_{2}
, denoted by
\gamma
, is given by

\cos\gamma=\frac{R^{2}+|\textbf{R}-\textbf{r}|^{2}-r^{2}}{2R|\textbf{R}-\textbf{r}|}\,.

(5)

The dipole arising from the kinetic mixing becomes,

\displaystyle\frac{\mu}{M\hat{\mu}}\textbf{p}_{1}\cdot\textbf{p}_{r}

\displaystyle=\mu(\dot{\textbf{r}}_{1}\cdot\dot{\textbf{r}}_{2}-\dot{\textbf{r}}^{2}_{1})

(6)

\displaystyle=\mu\left(\frac{M_{*}(R^{2}+|\textbf{R}-\textbf{r}|^{2}-r^{2})}{(R|\textbf{R}-\textbf{r}|)^{3/2}}-\frac{2M_{*}}{R}\right)

\displaystyle=\mu\left[\frac{M_{*}}{R}\left(2+\frac{\hat{\textbf{n}}\cdot\textbf{r}}{R}\right)-\frac{2M_{*}}{R}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=+\left(\frac{M_{*}\mu}{R}\right)\left(\frac{\hat{\textbf{n}}\cdot\textbf{r}}{R}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)^{2}\,.

As advertised, this precisely cancels the dipole contribution in (
3
).

Appendix 9.C
Tidal Moments of Inclined Orbits

We wish to describe the tidal moments
\mathcal{E}_{\ell_{*}m_{*}}
in (
2
) for general orbits.
In the usual angular spherical coordinates
\{\Theta_{*},\Phi_{*}\}
(see Fig.
2
), they are

\mathcal{E}_{\ell_{*}m_{*}}(\Theta_{*},\Phi_{*})=\frac{4\pi}{2\ell_{*}+1}Y^{*}_{\ell_{*}m_{*}}(\Theta_{*},\Phi_{*})\,,

(1)

where have suppress all explicit dependences on time
t
.
Although (
1
) provides a closed-form expression for the tidal moments for all
\ell_{*}
, these angular coordinates do not naturally adapt to the orbital motion of the binary. Instead, it is more convenient to express the tidal moments in terms of the inclination angle and the true anomaly
\{\iota_{*},\varphi_{*}\}
described in §
2
:

\mathcal{E}_{\ell_{*}m_{*}}(\iota_{*},\varphi_{*})=\sum_{m_{\varphi}=-\ell_{*}}^{\ell_{*}}\varepsilon^{(m_{\varphi})}_{\ell_{*}m_{*}}(\iota_{*})\,e^{-im_{\varphi}\varphi_{*}}\,,

(2)

where the reduced tidal moments,
\varepsilon_{\ell_{*}m_{*}}^{(m_{\varphi})}
, can be obtained by using the normalized binary separation vector
1
1

1

This expression for
\hat{\textbf{n}}
is obtained by applying the coordinate transformation (
1
) to its equivalent expression in the usual spherical coordinates,
\hat{\textbf{n}}=(\sin\Theta_{*}\cos\Phi_{*},\sin\Theta_{*}\sin\Phi_{*},\cos\Phi_{*})
.

\hat{\textbf{n}}=(\cos\iota_{*}\cos\varphi_{*},\sin\varphi_{*},\sin\iota_{*}\cos\varphi_{*})
in the symmetric-trace-free tensor representation of the gravitational perturbation (see e.g.
[
533
,
51
]
). The sizes of these reduced moments affect the magnitudes of the the overlap (
13
), while the oscillatory term
e^{-im_{\varphi}\varphi_{*}}
are shown explicitly in (
13
). Interestingly, the true anomaly
\varphi_{*}
only appears in the exponents of the oscillatory terms. On the other hand, the inclination angle
\iota_{*}
determines the strength of the perturbation and implicitly affects the summation over
m_{\varphi}
in (
2
). As we shall see, this means that
\iota_{*}
also determines the polarization of the perturbation.

It is instructive to illustrate the effect of
\iota_{*}
on the tidal moments through an explicit example. Let us therefore consider the quadrupole
\ell_{*}=2
, where the tidal moments are

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2\mp 2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{6\pi}{5}}\left(\cos\iota_{*}\cos\varphi_{*}\pm i\sin\varphi_{*}\right)^{2}\,,

(3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2\mp 1}

\displaystyle=\pm\sqrt{\frac{6\pi}{5}}\sin\iota_{*}\cos\varphi_{*}\left(\cos\iota_{*}\cos\varphi_{*}\pm i\sin\varphi_{*}\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{20}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{5}}\left(1-3\cos 2\varphi_{*}+6\cos^{2}\varphi_{*}\cos 2\iota_{*}\right)\,.

For equatorial orbits,
\iota_{*}=0
, this become

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2m_{*}}(\iota_{*}=0)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{6\pi}{5}}\left\{e^{+2i\varphi_{*}},\,0,\,-\sqrt{2/3},\,0,\,e^{-2i\varphi_{*}}\right\},

(4)

where the list runs from
m_{*}\in\{-2,\cdots\hskip-2.0pt,+2\}
. Since the tidal moments vanish for
m_{*}=\pm 1
, states with
\Delta m_{ab}=\pm 1
decouple from each other—see the selection rules (S1) and (V1) in §
2
. On the other hand, all of the non-vanishing components in (
4
) consists of a
single
oscillatory term
\propto e^{-im_{*}\varphi_{*}}
, with
m_{\varphi}=m_{*}
. These properties generalize to all values of
\ell_{*}
. In particular, for even (odd) values of
\ell_{*}
, the odd (even)
m_{*}
-th component vanishes, and each of the non-vanishing component is a single oscillatory term with
m_{\varphi}=m_{*}
. For instance, for
\ell_{*}=3
, we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{3m_{*}}(\iota_{*}=0)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{5\pi}{7}}

(5)

\displaystyle\times\left\{e^{+3i\varphi_{*}},\,0,\,-\sqrt{3/5}e^{+i\varphi_{*}},\,0,\,\sqrt{3/5}e^{-i\varphi_{*}},\,0,\,-e^{-3i\varphi_{*}}\right\}\,,

where states with
\Delta m_{ab}=\pm 1
are now connected through the weaker octupolar perturbation. This property can be seen most transparently by setting
\iota_{*}=0
in (
1
), where
\Phi_{*}=\varphi_{*}
and the spherical harmonics in (
1
) obeys
Y^{*}_{\ell_{*}m_{*}}\propto e^{-im_{*}\Phi_{*}}\propto e^{-im_{*}\varphi_{*}}
. Since the tidal moments for equatorial orbits induce perturbations with definite frequency
m_{*}\varphi_{*}
, these perturbations are said to be
circularly-polarized
.

The properties described above no longer hold for general inclined orbits with
\iota_{*}\neq 0
. For example, for
\iota_{*}=\pi/3
, (
3
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2\mp 2}(\iota_{*}=\pi/3)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{32}\sqrt{\frac{6\pi}{5}}\left(9e^{\pm 2i\varphi_{*}}+e^{\mp 2i\varphi_{*}}-6\right),

(6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2\mp 1}(\iota_{*}=\pi/3)

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{1}{16}\sqrt{\frac{18\pi}{5}}\left(3e^{\pm 2i\varphi_{*}}-e^{\mp 2i\varphi_{*}}+2\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{20}(\iota_{*}=\pi/3)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{48}\sqrt{\frac{9\pi}{5}}\left(9e^{+2i\varphi_{*}}+9e^{-2i\varphi_{*}}+2\right).

Not only are all of the tidal components now non-vanishing, they are given by a superposition of
\propto e^{\pm 2i\varphi_{*}}
and constant terms.
This feature can also be generalized to all
\ell_{*}
in inclined orbits, where the multipole moments consist of superpositions of oscillatory terms
\propto e^{-im_{\varphi}\varphi_{*}}
, with
m_{\varphi}
summed over all permissible odd (even) values for odd (even) values of
\ell_{*}
. In these cases, the off-diagonal terms in the Schrödinger frame Hamiltonian, such as (
4
), consist of a summation over different frequencies.
Furthermore, unlike for equatorial orbits, these tidal moments now necessarily contain oscillatory terms with opposite values of
\pm|m_{\varphi}|
. This is because the motion of an inclined orbit,
projected
onto the equatorial plane, can be represented by a superposition of co-rotating and counter-rotating orbits. Since the amplitudes of these oscillatory terms are different (except for
m_{*}=0
, which is azimuthal about the spin-axis of the cloud), the projected equatorial orbit still has definite orientation. Inclined orbits therefore generate
elliptically-polarized
gravitational perturbations.

Finally, in the special limit
\iota_{*}=\pi/2
, (
3
) turns into

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2m_{*}}(\iota_{*}=\pi/2)=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{6\pi}{5}}

(7)

\displaystyle\times\left\{-\sin^{2}\varphi_{*},\,i\sin 2\varphi_{*},\,(3\cos 2\varphi_{*}+1)/\sqrt{6},\,i\sin 2\varphi_{*},\,-\sin^{2}\varphi_{*}\right\}.

The amplitudes of both
\pm|m_{\varphi}|
oscillatory terms now have the same magnitude. In this case, the orbital motion projected onto the equatorial plane is just given by an oscillating perturbation along a straight line. By having the same magnitudes, the projected co-rotating and counter-rotating orbits cancel the net motion along the direction orthogonal to this straight line. The gravitational perturbation generated in this case is hence
linearly-polarized
. Our explicit example for the
\ell_{*}=2
tidal moment, from (
4
) to (
7
), represent the continuous behavior of
\mathcal{E}_{\ell_{*}m_{*}}
of all
\ell_{*}
under a rotation about
\iota_{*}
.

Appendix 9.D
Higher-Order Couplings

In §
2
, we ignored the
\alpha
-corrections to the gravitational potentials (
5
) and (
6
). However, these higher-order couplings can still mediate interesting transitions, especially if they mediate resonances that are forbidden by the selection rules of their leading-order counterparts.
We will now show the expressions of these additional couplings, leaving a detailed investigation of their effects for future work.

It is useful to first estimate the order in
\alpha
at which the
\alpha
-expansion should be truncated. As a useful guide, we require the Landau-Zener parameter (
13
) to be greater than order unity, such that the nature of the transitions are adiabatic.
Denoting the higher-order overlaps by
\eta_{\beta}\equiv\alpha^{\beta}\eta
, where
\eta
is the leading-order overlap, cf. (
13
), we find that

\frac{\eta_{\beta}^{2}}{\gamma}\simeq 2.8\times 10^{4}\frac{q}{(1+q)^{5/3}}\left(\frac{R_{ab}}{0.3}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{|\Delta m_{ab}|}{2}\right)^{5/3}\left|\frac{n_{a}^{2}n^{2}_{b}}{n^{2}_{a}-n_{b}^{2}}\right|^{5/3}\left(\frac{0.07}{\alpha}\right)^{5}\alpha^{2\beta}\,,

(1)

where we assumed the transitions are Bohr-like, and have used (
21
) and (
22
). By demanding adiabaticity in (
1
), i.e.
\eta_{\beta}^{2}/\gamma\gtrsim 1
, we find
\beta\lesssim 2
. This conclusion remains unchanged for fine and hyperfine transitions. In what follows, we will therefore expand the gravitational interactions up to order
\alpha^{2}
.

For the scalar cloud, the gravitational perturbation up to order
\alpha^{2}
is

\displaystyle V_{*}=-\frac{1}{4}\mu\bar{h}^{00}-i\bar{h}^{0i}\partial_{i}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{3})\,,

(2)

where the first term is the same as that in (
5
), and the second term arises from the (trace-reversed) gravitomagnetic perturbation
\bar{h}^{0i}
. The latter metric perturbation scales as
\bar{h}^{0i}\sim v\hskip 1.0pt\bar{h}^{00}
, where
v
is the typical velocity of the binary. Since
\partial_{i}\psi\sim\mu\alpha\psi
and
v\lesssim\alpha
(see Footnote
3
), this new term is
\alpha^{2}
-suppressed compared to its leading-order counterpart. On the other hand, for the vector cloud, we find that

\displaystyle V_{*}^{il}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\delta^{il}\mu\bar{h}^{00}-\frac{\bar{h}^{00}}{2\mu}\partial^{i}\partial^{l}-\frac{\partial_{r}\bar{h}^{00}}{4\mu}{\epsilon^{i}}_{rp}{\epsilon^{l}}_{pq}\partial_{q}

(3)

\displaystyle+\frac{i}{2}\partial^{l}\bar{h}^{0i}+\frac{i}{2}\bar{h}^{0l}\partial^{i}-i\delta^{il}\bar{h}^{0p}\partial_{p}\,,

where
\epsilon_{ijk}
is the Levi-Civita symbol, and we replace all of the temporal components
\psi^{0}
with the Lorenz condition
\partial_{i}\psi_{i}+i\mu\psi_{0}=\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{3})
. To obtain both (
2
) and (
3
), we utilized the fact that the gravitomagnetic perturbation is divergence-less, i.e.
\partial_{i}\bar{h}^{i0}=0
. We also simplified these results by ignoring higher-order corrections that contain time-derivatives,
\partial_{0}\psi\sim\mu\alpha^{2}\psi
, as the types of resonances that they mediate are already accessible by the leading-order potentials. This is to be contrasted with terms with spatial gradients, which change the angular structures of the field and therefore induce resonances that obey different selection rules.

Landau-Zener Transition

As discussed in detail in Section
3
, a key feature of the evolution of boson clouds in the inspiral of a binary are resonant Landau-Zener transitions.
In this appendix, we collect details of these transitions that are relevant to, but not necessarily suitable for, the main text. Specifically, in Appendix
10.A
, we first review the exact solution of the two-state Landau-Zener transition, cf.
[
441
,
442
]
, which provides intuition for understanding the more complicated multi-state transitions. In Appendix
10.B
, we then describe adiabatic Floquet theory. This is necessary for generalizing the dressed frame presented in Section
3
to connect the dynamics of a general binary-cloud system to those of a series of LZ transitions.

Appendix 10.A
The Two-State Model

The Schrödinger equation for the two-state LZ transition presented in §
1
can be reduced to the Weber equation of a quantum harmonic oscillator, and is thus exactly solvable. In the following, we will review this solution.

The dressed frame coefficients
d_{1}(t)
and
d_{2}(t)
evolve according to the Hamiltonian (
4
):

\displaystyle i\dot{d}_{1}

\displaystyle=+\tilde{\gamma}td_{1}/2+\eta d_{2}\,,

(1)

\displaystyle i\dot{d}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\tilde{\gamma}td_{2}/2+\eta d_{1}\,,

where we defined
\tilde{\gamma}\equiv|\Delta m|\gamma
. These equations can be combined into
the Weber equations for either coefficient,

\displaystyle\ddot{d}_{1}+\tfrac{1}{4}\left((\tilde{\gamma}t)^{2}+2i\tilde{\gamma}+4\eta^{2}\right)d_{1}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(2)

\displaystyle\ddot{d}_{2}+\tfrac{1}{4}\left((\tilde{\gamma}t)^{2}-2i\tilde{\gamma}+4\eta^{2}\right)d_{2}

\displaystyle=0\,,

whose solutions are the parabolic cylinder functions
[
439
]
,

\displaystyle d_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=C_{1}\,{\mathrm{D}}_{-iz}\left((-1)^{1/4}\sqrt{\tilde{\gamma}}t\right)+C_{2}\,{\mathrm{D}}_{iz-1}\left((-1)^{3/4}\sqrt{\tilde{\gamma}}t\right),

(3)

\displaystyle d_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{1/4}}{\sqrt{z}}C_{2}\,{\mathrm{D}}_{iz}\left((-1)^{3/4}\sqrt{\tilde{\gamma}}t\right)

\displaystyle+(-1)^{1/4}\sqrt{z}\,C_{1}\,{\mathrm{D}}_{-iz-1}\left((-1)^{1/4}\sqrt{\tilde{\gamma}}t\right),

where we have defined
z\equiv\eta^{2}/\tilde{\gamma}
and related the two solutions using (
1
). We are ultimately interested in finding the total population contained in the second state
|d_{2}(t)|^{2}
in the asymptotic future, assuming that the system fully occupied the first state in the asymptotic past,
{|d_{1}(-\infty)|^{2}=1}
. Imposing this initial condition, the undetermined coefficients in (
3
) are (up to an arbitrary choice of phase)

C_{1}=\exp\Big(\frac{3\pi}{4}z\Big)\quad\text{and}\quad C_{2}=\frac{i\sqrt{2\pi}}{\Gamma(iz)}\exp\Big(\frac{\pi}{4}z\Big)\,.

(4)

We thus find that

\displaystyle|d_{1}(\infty)|^{2}

\displaystyle=e^{-2\pi z}\,,

(5)

\displaystyle|d_{2}(\infty)|^{2}

\displaystyle=1-e^{-2\pi z}\,.

We see that for large values of the Landau-Zener parameter,
z=\eta^{2}/\tilde{\gamma}\gg 1
,
the “probability” that the system remains in the state
|1\rangle
is exponentially small
and the state
|2\rangle
becomes almost fully occupied.

Likewise, if we impose
|d_{2}(-\infty)|^{2}=1
, then the coefficients in (
3
) are (up to an arbitrary phase)

C_{1}=0\quad\text{and}\quad C_{2}=z\exp\Big(\hskip-2.0pt-\frac{\pi}{4}z\Big)\,,

(6)

and the asymptotic occupations are the converse of (
5
):

\displaystyle|d_{1}(\infty)|^{2}

\displaystyle=1-e^{-2\pi z}\,,

(7)

\displaystyle|d_{2}(\infty)|^{2}

\displaystyle=e^{-2\pi z}\,.

As discussed in §
3
, the information contained in (
5
) and (
7
) can be encoded in an S-matrix,
|\psi(\infty)\rangle=S|\psi(-\infty)\rangle
. We will often only consider the modulus of the S-matrix elements

|S|=\begin{pmatrix}e^{-\pi z}&\displaystyle\sqrt{1-e^{-2\pi z}}\\
\displaystyle\sqrt{1-e^{-2\pi z}}&e^{-\pi z}\end{pmatrix},

(8)

which have well-defined asymptotic limits.

Appendix 10.B
Adiabatic Floquet Theory

Throughout Section
3
, it was convenient to work in a dressed frame that rotated along with the orbital motion of the gravitational perturbation, making it clear that the system could evolve adiabatically. However, states are generically connected by perturbations that oscillate at multiple frequencies, especially when we consider more general inspiral configurations with eccentric and inclined orbits, cf. Appendix
9.C
. In that case, there are multiple dressed frames and one would have to awkwardly switch between them depending on which resonance one is interested in. Fortunately, the Floquet Hamiltonian provides a natural generalization of the dressed frame to arbitrary inspiral configurations, and we now review its construction.

Floquet theory relies on the existence of a compact variable or phase to trivialize some part of the dynamics. Throughout, we will concentrate on the Hamiltonian studied in the main text,

\mathcal{H}_{ab}(t,\varphi_{*}(t))=E_{a}\delta_{ab}+\sum_{m_{\varphi}\in\mathbb{Z}}\eta_{ab}^{(m_{\varphi})}(t)e^{-im_{\varphi}\varphi_{*}(t)}\,,

(1)

where we now emphasize its dependence on the angular variable
\varphi_{*}(t)
. This Hamiltonian describes generic orbits, with inclination and eccentricity. Intuitively, it would be useful to expand in the Fourier modes
e^{ik\varphi_{*}(t)}
, as this will trivialize the oscillatory part of the dynamics. This is how Floquet theory is typically presented—if a Hamiltonian is time-periodic,
\mathcal{H}(t)=\mathcal{H}(t+2\pi)
, we may expand the wavefunction as
\psi(x,t)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\psi_{k}(x,t)e^{ikt}
to derive a “Floquet Hamiltonian” for the modes
\psi_{k}(x,t)
that does not depend on time. However, it is not clear what the Fourier modes are for the phase
\varphi_{*}(t)
, which can be an arbitrary non-monotonic function of time. It will thus be useful to present an alternative construction of the Floquet counterpart of (
1
), which is more thoroughly reviewed in
[
534
]
.

It will be useful to extend (
1
) to a family of Hamiltonians
\mathcal{H}_{ab}(t,\varphi_{*}(t)+\theta)
, parameterized by a new phase
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
, each with its own time evolution operator

i\hskip 0.5pt\partial_{t}U(t,t_{0};\theta)=\mathcal{H}(t,\varphi_{*}(t)+\theta)U(t,t_{0};\theta)\,.

(2)

This is useful because we can then extend our original Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}
to
\mathscr{K}=\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{L}
, where
\mathscr{L}=L_{2}(S_{1},\mathrm{d}\theta/2\pi)
is the space of square-integrable
2\pi
-periodic functions. This space is generated by
e^{ik\theta}
, with
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. That is, we now think of
\theta
as an additional variable in our problem. The dressed-frame coefficients then become functions of this variable,
c_{a}(t)\to c_{a}(t,\theta)
, and inner products between two states are defined by

\langle\psi_{1}|\psi_{2}\rangle=\sum_{a}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\!\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi}c_{1,a}^{*}(t,\theta)c_{2,a}(t,\theta)\,,

(3)

where
|\psi_{i}\rangle\equiv\sum_{a}c_{i,a}(t,\theta)|a\rangle
.
Time evolution is again generated by
U(t,t_{0};\theta)
, such that

c_{a}(t,\theta)=U_{ab}(t,t_{0};\theta)c_{b}(t_{0},\theta)\,,

(4)

where the time-evolution operator has been extended to the new Hilbert space
\mathscr{K}
by treating its dependence on
\theta
as multiplication.

The reason this is useful is that we may now define a phase translation operator

\mathcal{T}_{\phi}=\exp(\phi\,\partial_{\theta})\,,

(5)

which acts on a state
\psi(t,\theta)
like
\mathcal{T}_{\phi}\psi(t,\theta)=\psi(t,\theta+\phi)
. Crucially, this operator allows us to remove the dependence of
\varphi_{*}(t)
of the Hamiltonian (
1
):

\mathcal{H}(t,\varphi_{*}(t)+\theta)=\mathcal{T}_{\varphi_{*}(t)}\mathcal{H}(t,\theta)\mathcal{T}_{-\varphi_{*}(t)}\,.

(6)

This allows us to rewrite the full time evolution operator in terms of the
Floquet time evolution operator

U(t,t_{0};\theta)=\mathcal{T}_{\varphi_{*}(t)}U_{\mathcal{K}}(t,t_{0})\mathcal{T}_{-\varphi_{*}(t_{0})}\,,

(7)

which evolves
i\,\partial_{t}U_{\mathcal{K}}(t,t_{0})=\mathcal{K}(t,\theta)U_{\mathcal{K}}(t,t_{0})
according to the
Floquet Hamiltonian

\mathcal{K}_{ab}(t,\theta)=\delta_{ab}\big(E_{a}-i\dot{\varphi}_{*}(t)\partial_{\theta}\big)+\sum_{m_{\varphi}\in\mathbb{Z}}\eta_{ab}^{(m_{\varphi})}(t)\,e^{-im_{\varphi}\theta}\,.

(8)

The transformation (
6
) and the Hamiltonian (
8
) generalize the dressed frame transformation (
25
) and the Hamiltonian (
26
), respectively. All of the “fast” motion due to
\varphi_{*}(t)
has now been removed from (
8
), which evolves slowly in time.

Given a solution of the Schrödinger equation

i\,\partial_{t}c_{a}(t,\theta)=\mathcal{K}_{ab}(t,\theta)c_{b}(t,\theta)\,,

(9)

we can then generate a solution for the original Hamiltonian by simple substitution,
c_{a}(t)=c_{a}(t,\varphi_{*}(t))
. If we expand
c_{a}(t,\theta)
into the Floquet eigenbasis
e^{ik\theta}
,

c_{a}(t,\theta)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}c_{a}^{(k)}(t)e^{ik\theta}\,,

(10)

then it is clear that this substitution is simply the Fourier expansion in
\varphi_{*}(t)
we originally wanted.

It is particularly useful to analyze the dynamics in this Floquet basis, in which the Floquet Hamiltonian is

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{ab}^{f_{1},f_{2}}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\!\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi}\,e^{if_{1}\theta}\mathcal{K}_{ab}(t,\theta)e^{-if_{2}\theta}

(11)

\displaystyle=\delta_{ab}\delta_{f_{1},f_{2}}\left(E_{a}-f_{2}\dot{\varphi}_{*}(t)\right)+\eta_{ab}^{(f_{1}-f_{2})}(t)\,.

Clearly, this is of the same form as the dressed Hamiltonian we considered in Section
3
, and the same decoupling arguments apply here. Indeed, we may consider the instantaneous (quasi)-energy eigenstates and describe the cloud’s evolution throughout the entire inspiral as a series of isolated scattering events. Crucially, if the system is in a state with definite Floquet number, the translation in
\theta
does not affect populations,
|c_{a}(t,\varphi_{*}(t))|^{2}=|c_{a}(t,\theta)|^{2}
. We can avoid working with the Schrödinger-frame coefficients entirely, if we are only interested in the population contained within a state.

Backreaction on the Orbit

Our analysis of the backreaction in §
1
relied crucially on the balance of angular momentum between the orbit and the cloud. In this appendix, we provide a more detailed analysis of this balance. We first derive the cloud-orbit coupling through conservation of angular momentum, and then analyze the orbit’s behavior during an adiabatic transition. We find that angular momentum conservation during the transition can cause the orbit to either float or kick.

Appendix 11.A
Orbital Dynamics

The motion of the binary companion satisfies (see e.g.
[
535
,
536
]
)

\displaystyle\ddot{R}_{*}-R_{*}\Omega^{2}=-\frac{(1+q)M}{R_{*}^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt+\frac{64q(1+q)M^{3}}{15}\frac{\dot{R}_{*}}{R_{*}^{4}}+\frac{16qM^{2}}{5}\frac{\dot{R}^{3}_{*}}{R_{*}^{3}}+\frac{16qM^{2}}{5R_{*}}\dot{R}_{*}\Omega^{2}\,,\quad

(1)

\displaystyle R_{*}^{2}\dot{\Omega}+2R_{*}\dot{R}_{*}\Omega=-\frac{24q(1+q)M^{3}}{5R_{*}^{2}}\Omega-\frac{8qM^{2}}{5R_{*}}\dot{R}_{*}^{2}\Omega-\frac{8qM^{2}}{5}R_{*}\Omega^{3}\,,

(2)

where we have included the leading Newtonian and radiation reaction forces. Both the radial separation,
R_{*}=R_{*}(t)
, and instantaneous frequency,
\Omega=\Omega(t)
, are functions of time and, since the orbital plane does not precess, they fully describe the orbital motion. We should interpret (
2
) as a statement of angular momentum conservation: the orbital angular momentum of the binary on the left-hand side decreases due to gravitational-wave emission on the right-hand side.

Fortunately, most of the terms in (
1
) and (
2
) can be dropped for the large, quasi-circular orbits we consider. Let us consider such an orbit of characteristic size
R_{r}
and frequency
\Omega_{r}
. We take the frequency to be near a Bohr resonance,
\Omega_{r}\sim\mu\alpha^{2}
. Because the orbit is quasi-circular, we have
\Omega_{r}^{2}R_{r}^{3}=(1+q)M
, and so the dimensionless quantity

\varpi\equiv\frac{2q^{1/5}R_{r}\Omega_{r}}{(1+q)^{2/5}}\propto\frac{q^{1/5}\alpha}{(1+q)^{1/15}}\,,

(3)

is suppressed
\alpha\ll 1
.
Because the orbit changes on the timescale of order
\Omega_{r}/\gamma
, where
\gamma
was defined in (
16
), it will also be useful to define the dimensionless time

\tau\equiv\frac{\gamma t}{\Omega_{r}}\,.

(4)

Finally, we define the dimensionless radius and frequency

R_{*}(t)=R_{r}\hskip 0.5ptr_{*}(\tau)\quad\text{and}\quad\Omega_{*}(t)=\Omega_{r}\hskip 0.5pt\omega_{*}(\tau)\,,

(5)

so that the equations of motion become

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=1-\omega_{*}^{2}r_{*}^{3}+\frac{\varpi^{10}}{50}\left(18r^{2}r_{*}^{\prime\prime}-\frac{(4+3\omega_{*}^{2}r_{*}^{3})r_{*}^{\prime}}{r_{*}^{2}}\right)-\frac{27\varpi^{20}}{1250}\frac{r_{*}^{\prime 3}}{r_{*}}\,,

(6)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\omega_{*}^{\prime}+\frac{2\omega_{*}r_{*}^{\prime}}{r^{*}}+\frac{\omega_{*}(3+\omega_{*}^{2}r_{*}^{3})}{12r_{*}^{4}}+\frac{3\varpi^{10}}{100}\frac{\omega_{*}r_{*}^{\prime 2}}{r_{*}^{3}}\,,

(7)

where primes denote derivatives with respect to the time
\tau
.
Clearly, the terms dressed by (high) powers of

This suppression is lost for extremely large mass ratios. However, for

\Omega^{2}R_{*}^{3}=(1+q)M\,,
